that tracks how each handshake nudges Rcoh and hence the local lapse function.

Unified Picture
Both macroscopic entropy gradients and microscopic handshakes move the same dynami￾cal variable. Increasing Rcoh thickens local time (ρt ↑) and deepens curvature, while any
mismatch in phase—no matter whether generated thermally (Verlinde) or by incomplete
handshake cycles (RTI)—drains ρt through the friction term and restores the classical arrow
of time. The entropic and transactional narratives merge into the statement that informa￾tional coincidence drives geometry.
Quantum Coherence as Information Density
Inside this architecture coherence takes the role once reserved for Shannon bits. A spatial
region whose field modes share a common phase enjoys a high value of Rcoh; its local infor￾mation density is therefore large. Because the master action rewards phase alignment by
lowering potential energy, coherent patches attract additional energy until damping counters
the inflow. The trade–off between amplitude and frequency at fixed energy, A2ω

means that a high–amplitude, phase–locked region oscillates more slowly; the attendant

Astrophysical Echoes and Quasicrystal Order

higher–dimensional periodic order, exactly as the spatial distribution of coherence–voids and
coherence–clumps in the cosmic web is interpreted here as a projection of high-dimensional
informational curvature. Regions of constructive interference collapse into galaxies and black

.
2.1 Operational and Mathematical Definitions of Primitive Fields
To ensure clarity and empirical accessibility, we now define the theory’s two fundamental

meaning..
(1) The Complex Coherence Field, ψ(x): The foundational axiomatic postulate of
Super Information Theory is the existence of a single, dynamical complex scalar field, ψ(x),
which serves as the universal informational substrate. It is defined at every point on the
spacetime manifold and possesses a local U(1) gauge symmetry. Its phase, θ(x) ≡ arg(ψ(x)),
defines the gauge connection from which electromagnetism emerges. All dynamics in SIT
are ultimately governed by the evolution of this field.
(2) The Coherence Ratio, Rcoh(x):

3 Operational Definitions and the Informational Fields
A central pillar of this theory is that “information” is not an abstract mathematical construct,
but a physically real, operationally defined property of physical systems. Here, information is
defined as the local coincidence of independent events—a concept that is both experimentally
accessible and fundamentally general.
3.1 Information Density as a Local Field
We can formalize this by defining the coincidence field C(x) as the rate or density of
coincidence events at each spacetime point x. The local information density I(x) is then a
monotonic function of this field, I(x) = f(C(x)).
This operational definition ensures that information is a field-theoretic, measurable, and
causal quantity. The fundamental fields of SIT, the complex coherence field ψ(x) and the
time-density ρt(x), are the formal mathematical objects that quantify the structure and
dynamics of these coincidence events.
• The modulus |ψ(x)| ≡ Rcoh(x) quantifies the degree of structured phase alignment or
correlation between events.
• The time-density ρt(x) quantifies the local rate of these events.
This framework grounds all higher-order informational constructs—such as mutual informa￾tion and entropy—in the local, measurable substrate of physical coincidence.
3.2 Summary of Operational Definitions
The operational definitions and measurable constructs developed in previous sections (no￾tably the time-density field ρt and coherence ratio Rcoh) provide a robust foundation for
cross-disciplinary translation. SIT’s fields, while mathematically explicit, can also be under￾stood in terms of established concepts from neuroscience and information theory:
• Coincidence as a Bit: Drawing on Blumberg’s early work, SIT interprets the basic
bit of information—not as a binary voltage or spin, but as a “coincidence pattern”: a
spatiotemporal confluence of signals or events. Operationally, the detection of coinci￾dence (in neural or quantum contexts) corresponds to an increase in local Rcoh.
23

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 24 / 198>

• Self Aware Networks: SIT extends this further, adopting the view that oscilla￾tory synchrony and phase wave differentials in biological networks instantiate high￾coherence states, thereby realizing physical bits as emergent, distributed order param￾eters. These can be measured experimentally (e.g., with EEG, MEG, or multi-electrode
neural recordings).
• Coherence as a Physical Order Parameter: In quantum systems, Rcoh maps
onto the degree of superposition or entanglement; in neural systems, it quantifies syn￾chronous firing or phase-locking; in information theory, it encodes mutual information
or entropy reduction.
3.3 The Coherence-Time Law and Emergent Gravity
Super-Information Theory interprets local quantum coherence as the single source of mass,
energy and gravitational phenomena. The canonical formulation of general relativity asserts
that all forms of energy and momentum, including electromagnetic radiation, source gravi￾tational fields through the stress-energy tensor Tµν. Under this paradigm, the gravitational
field of light is determined solely by its energy density, with no explicit reference to quantum
coherence. Yet, Super Information Theory (SIT) posits a more radical connection: that
quantum coherence itself is not only informationally fundamental, but physically causal in
the generation and modulation of gravity.
In SIT, the relationship between information and gravity is direct and causal. The core
mechanism can be summarized in three points:
1. Mass from high coherence. Regions of elevated phase alignment act as “stiff”
pockets in the coherence field; the resistance they offer to decoherence appears macro￾scopically as inertial and gravitational mass.
2. Energy as dynamical flow. Energy measures the capacity of a system to reorganize
its internal phase pattern. Stable coherence locks in rest energy (E = mc2
), while
interactions redistribute coherence and hence energy between subsystems.
3. Gravity from time-density gradients. Spatial variation in ρt alters local clock
rates; neighboring regions with different rates see trajectories bend exactly as predicted
by curved-spacetime general relativity. In SIT, curvature is therefore an emergent
manifestation of a coherence gradient rather than an independent geometric ingredient.
In short, dense, phase-locked coherence slows time and appears as mass; gradients in
that density redirect neighboring clocks and mimic curvature; the flows of coherence that
rearrange these patterns are what we call energy. All three classical quantities thus stem
from a single informational substrate.
3.4 Relativistic Clarification: Internal vs. External Coherence Per￾spectives

small, ∆E large), implying fast decoherence. However, in the particle’s own dilated proper

informational synchronisation that ties the quantum time–energy uncertainty ∆E ∆t ≥ ℏ/2
to local variations in ρt

3.5 Determining the Functional Form Linking Coherence–Decoherence
to Local Time Density
A key challenge within Super Information Theory (SIT) is determining the precise mathemat￾ical relationship connecting the coherence–decoherence ratio (Rcoh) to the local time density
field (ρt). Integrating insights from deterministic wave dynamics (SuperTimePosition), wave￾based thermodynamics (Micah’s New Law of Thermodynamics), and quantum–gravitational
interference (Super Dark Time), we propose a refined theoretical framework clearly linking
these concepts.
3.6 Synchronization and Informational Coherence

3.10 Information as Active Substrate
Information is treated as ontologically active. A spatial gradient in Rcoh establishes a local
vector potential, while a temporal gradient in ρt deforms proper time. The two gradients
together fix the phase of quantum states, the curvature of spacetime, and the flow of macro￾scopic entropy. In this framework, information is elevated from a bookkeeping device to a
physical medium that shapes matter and energy.
3.11 Coherence as Informational Teamwork
Super Information Theory (SIT) conceives quantum coherence not as an individual property
of isolated particles but as a collective “teamwork” process, akin to how a phased array
channels random scatter into a focused beam. When many quantum states align their phases,
they form an informational blueprint that far exceeds the sum of separate contributions.
At the field-theoretic level, this teamwork can be encoded by a self-interacting term in the
potential for the coherence field, U(|ψ|), such as a quartic term λ

. This term provides

each other, yielding a coherence amplitude that grows faster than linearly with the number
of participants. The resultant coherence—and hence the enhancement of the time-density
field ρt—can scale superadditively. Physically, assembling quantum states into a coherent
configuration transforms incoherent scattering into a unified informational “signal,” boosting
ρt and generating a stronger emergent gravitational pull.
This same teamwork underlies measurement-induced collapse: previously decoherent
modes synchronize their phases through mutual interactions and informational exchange,
producing a collective realignment of the coherence field that manifests as an apparent in￾stantaneous “collapse” and a localized increase in ρt
.

4 The Coherence Field as a Memory Substrate
A foundational ontological claim of Super Information Theory is that the universe is not
merely governed by timeless laws, but actively records its own history within the geometric
structure of the coherence field. SIT posits that the complex coherence field, ψ(x), is not a
static background but a dynamic, path-dependent medium. This concept of informational
hysteresis—the dependence of the field’s current state on its prior evolution—provides a
direct, physical mechanism for the emergence of irreversibility and the observable arrow of
time.
4.1 Informational Hysteresis: The “Scar” of Interaction
Every physical interaction, from a quantum measurement to the collision of particles, leaves a
permanent, albeit subtle, modification—a “scar”—on the local configuration of the coherence
field. The evolution of the ψ field is fundamentally path-dependent; a system cannot return
to its exact prior state after undergoing a cycle of coherence and subsequent decoherence
because the informational landscape itself has been irreversibly altered. This stands in stark
contrast to frameworks where physical law is purely symmetric with respect to time reversal.
In SIT, while the laws are symmetric, the state of the physical substrate is not.
The universe cannot ‘un-decohere’ a region and return to a previous state, because the
field configuration that would be the starting point for the reversed process no longer exists.
Each event leaves an indelible, albeit subtle, trace in the informational substrate.
This principle explains why macroscopic processes are irreversible. The initial conditions
for a reversed process are physically inaccessible, as they have been overwritten by the
informational record of the forward process.
4.2 Holonomy and the Geometric Structure of the Past
The arrow of time emerges directly from the accumulating geometric memory of the coherence
field. In this framework, the past is not merely a sequence of bygone events but a persistent,
geometric “twist” in the informational fabric, mathematically described by the concept of
holonomy. Just as walking a closed loop on a curved surface can result in a net change of
direction, the cyclical evolution of the coherence field’s phase, θ(x), encodes a non-trivial
memory of the path taken.
This accumulating holonomy acts as an active constraint on future evolution, creating
a definitive one-way flow of time. The structure of the past is thus woven into the very
geometry of the present, shaping the dynamics of what is to come.
The field itself is creating the memory that defines the past and shapes the future, creating
that one-way arrow of time. It’s not the particle interactions themselves that are irreversible,
but how they affect the larger informational field.
Therefore, SIT elevates the arrow of time from a statistical mystery to a deterministic
and geometric consequence of a universe that perpetually remembers itself.

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 30 / 198>

5 Action Principle and Field Equations
5.1 Symmetries and Constraints on the Action
Super Information Theory is built on two primary fields: the complex coherence field ψ(x)
and the real time-density field ρt(x). To construct a physically viable theory, the action
governing these fields must respect fundamental symmetries, including general covariance
(diffeomorphism invariance) and local U(1) gauge invariance associated with the coherence
phase. Furthermore, to ensure causality and stability, we exclude higher-derivative operators
that lead to Ostrogradsky instabilities. These constraints lead to a unique minimal effective
action.
5.2 Symmetries and Gauge Structure
Super Information Theory (SIT) is built upon two primitive fields defined over a Lorentzian
spacetime manifold M with metric gµν: - The complex coherence field ψ(x), a fundamental
complex scalar. Its modulus, |ψ(x)| ≡ Rcoh(x), is a dimensionless, gauge-invariant observable
that quantifies the degree of local quantum coherence. - The time-density field ρt(x), a
fundamental real scalar with dimension [T
−1
], encoding the local density of event cycles.
The dynamics of these fields, as described by the SIT action, must respect the following
fundamental symmetries:
1. Diffeomorphism Invariance The action must be invariant under smooth coordinate
transformations x
µ → x
′µ
(x). This principle of general covariance dictates that all terms in
the action are constructed from scalars and covariant tensor contractions.
2. Local U(1) Gauge Invariance The fundamental complex field ψ(x) possesses a local
U(1) gauge symmetry, transforming as:
ψ(x) → e
iα(x)ψ(x),
for an arbitrary smooth function α(x). The action must be invariant under this trans￾formation. This requires that all potentials and couplings involving ψ depend only on its
gauge-invariant modulus, |ψ(x)|, and that all derivatives of ψ appear as gauge-covariant
derivatives, ensuring the phase θ(x) = arg(ψ(x)) transforms correctly as a connection.
3. Discrete Symmetries Time-reversal T, parity P, and charge conjugation C sym￾metries restrict the presence of terms odd under these transformations, forbidding CPT￾violating operators unless explicitly motivated.
4. Causality and Locality The action must produce hyperbolic, causal equations of
motion, prohibiting nonlocal terms or higher-derivative operators that generate Ostrogradsky
instabilities.

5.4 Exclusion of Additional Terms
To maintain physical viability and consistency, we exclude terms as follows:
Higher-Derivative Operators: Terms involving more than second-order derivatives, such
as (□ρt)

Non-Gauge-Invariant Terms: Any operators violating the local U(1) gauge symmetry,
such as explicit dependence on the coherence phase θ(x) without gauge-covariant derivatives,
are forbidden.

Nonlocal and Acausal Terms: Operators introducing explicit nonlocality or acausal
behavior are prohibited, preserving causality and locality at the fundamental level.
Redundant Operators: Terms that can be removed by field redefinitions, integrations
by parts, or use of equations of motion are omitted to ensure a minimal, irreducible action.

5.5 Motivation for the Action’s Form
Given the symmetry and stability constraints outlined above, the SIT action (1) represents
the unique, minimal effective action describing the coupled dynamics of the complex
coherence field ψ(x) and the time-density field ρt(x). Any significant deviation from this
form would violate a fundamental principle:
• Adding non-covariant terms would violate diffeomorphism invariance.
• Adding terms that depend explicitly on the phase θ(x) (rather than through gauge￾covariant derivatives) would violate local U(1) gauge invariance.
• Adding higher-derivative terms (e.g., (□ψ)
2
) would introduce Ostrogradsky insta￾bilities, violating causality and stability.
• Adding non-polynomial or non-local terms would jeopardize renormalizability (as an
effective field theory) and locality.
Therefore, the presented action is not an arbitrary choice but is the simplest, most robust
theoretical structure that can be built from the primitive fields ψ(x) and ρt(x) consistent
with the foundational principles of modern physics. This reasoning justifies treating ψ and ρt
as the fundamental dynamical fields mediating the unified quantum-gravitational dynamics
in SIT.

5.6.4 Physical Interpretation of Coherence Effects
A key conceptual result of SIT is that the coherence–decoherence ratio Rcoh controls how ρt
affects local curvature:
• High coherence (Rcoh → 1) amplifies the contribution of ρt to Tµν, effectively increasing
the local energy density and pressure. This can enhance gravitational binding.
• Strong decoherence (Rcoh → 0) suppresses the contribution of ρt
, diminishing its effec￾tive energy content in Tµν.
Thus, the SIT framework encodes quantum-informational effects into gravitational dynamics
by making the gravitational field dependent on the coherence properties of quantum states.
5.6.5 Recovery of General Relativity in the Low-Energy Limit
In the low-energy (classical) limit, where fluctuations of ρt are small and the field is nearly
constant, we have:
∂µρt ≈ 0, f1(ρt), f2(ρt) ≈ const.
The additional SIT terms in the stress-energy tensor become negligible, and the field equa￾tions reduce to the standard Einstein field equations of general relativity. This consistency
check ensures that SIT predictions agree with all well-tested gravitational phenomena.
5.6.6 Illustrative Examples
• Black Hole Thermodynamics. Near an event horizon, large quantum coherence in
field modes can significantly modify the local stress-energy distribution via ρt
, poten￾tially altering Hawking radiation rates and the near-horizon geometry.
• Cosmological Implications. On cosmological scales, a slowly varying ρt field can
mimic effects attributed to dark matter or dark energy. For instance, a spatial gra￾dient in ρt can manifest as an effective pressure, offering alternative explanations for
cosmological observations.

6 The Law of Coherence Conservation: From U(1)
Symmetry to a Universal Principle
Super Information Theory (SIT) asserts that coherence is the universal currency of informa￾tion across all physical substrates, from quantum systems to biological neural networks. The
conservation of coherence—its transformation and redistribution, rather than annihilation—
is posited as a foundational law, underpinning both the Heisenberg uncertainty principle in
quantum mechanics and the informational dynamics of complex systems such as the brain.
This section formalizes and generalizes the abstract law of coherence conservation, drawing a
continuous line between the quantum and neural realms. In this section, we explicitly derive
the Noether currents and conservation laws associated with the key symmetries of the Super
Information Theory (SIT) action. This addresses both general covariance, internal phase
symmetries, and any emergent conservation principles relevant for Rcoh(x) and ρt(x).
6.1 General Covariance and Energy–Momentum Conservation
The SIT action is constructed to be generally covariant:
SSIT =

6.2 Global U(1) Phase Symmetry and Coherence Current
The SIT action is constructed to be invariant under a global U(1) phase rotation of the
fundamental complex coherence field, ψ(x):
ψ(x) → e
iαψ(x),
where α is a constant real phase. This symmetry is guaranteed if the potential and coupling
terms in the action depend only on the gauge-invariant modulus, |ψ(x)| ≡ Rcoh(x).
35

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 36 / 198>

Noether Procedure: The relevant kinetic term in the Lagrangian for the complex field
ψ is:

of quantum coherence.
Physical Interpretation and Clarification of Coherence Conservation: The con￾servation law ∇µJ
µ
coh = 0 applies rigorously to the fundamental complex field ψ. It means
coherence behaves like a conserved fluid; it is never created or destroyed in a closed system,
only redistributed. This has crucial implications for understanding decoherence.
What ”Coherence Conservation” Means:
• The total coherence within a fully isolated (closed) system is constant.
• Coherence can flow from one region to another, leading to wave-like redistribution.
What ”Coherence Conservation” Does Not Mean:
• It does not prevent local coherence from decreasing. What is commonly called ”de￾coherence” in an open system is the process of coherence flowing from the system
into its unobserved environment. The conservation law is not violated for the total
system-plus-environment.
• An explicit interaction term with an environment acts as an effective source or sink for
the subsystem’s coherence, even while the global current remains conserved.
True breaking of the fundamental symmetry would require terms in the action that explicitly
depend on the phase of ψ, which are excluded by construction.
6.3 Coherence as the Fundamental Informational Quantity
In the language of SIT, coherence refers to the structured, phase-related alignment of states—
be it the off-diagonal elements of a quantum density matrix or the phase-locked oscillations
of neural populations. Information is instantiated, preserved, and transmitted through the
existence and manipulation of these coherent patterns.
Let C represent a quantitative measure of coherence in a given domain (e.g., position,
momentum, frequency, amplitude). Unlike energy or entropy, coherence is inherently rela￾tional: it depends not only on the population statistics of states but on the precise phase
relationships that bind them into functional wholes.
36

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 37 / 198>

6.4 Formal Statement of Coherence Conservation
The abstract law of coherence conservation is simply stated: Coherence cannot be created or
destroyed in the act of measurement or transformation; it can only be redistributed between
complementary domains.
Mathematically, for any pair of complementary observables (A, B), there exists a con￾straint such that:
CA CB ≳ χ, (12)
where χ is a domain-dependent constant reflecting the minimum irreducible product (as in
the uncertainty relation, where χ = ℏ/2). This relationship expresses that the extraction of
maximal coherence (precise information) in observable A necessitates a corresponding loss
of coherence (increased uncertainty) in observable B. The product or sum of coherence
measures across all complementary domains is conserved or bounded.
This law is not limited to the quantum scale. In all information-processing systems,
coherence is the carrier of distinguishability, meaning, and memory. Its conservation governs
the transformation of information under all physical laws.
6.5 Quantum Systems: Measurement and Redistribution
In quantum mechanics, coherence conservation is embodied in the dynamics of measure￾ment and state evolution. A position measurement that collapses a wavefunction to a sharp
peak (maximal Cx) necessarily induces maximal spread (minimal Cp) in the momentum do￾main. Unitary evolution preserves the total quantum coherence, redistributing it among
the system’s degrees of freedom, while measurement represents the selective extraction (and
apparent loss) of coherence in a particular basis, balanced by a corresponding decoherence
in the conjugate basis.
6.6 Neural Systems: Oscillatory Dynamics and Informational Flow
In neural systems, the same law operates at the level of population dynamics and oscillatory
synchrony. Extraction of precise, high-frequency information (e.g., gamma-band synchrony
during sensory encoding or attention) is invariably associated with reduced coherence in
other frequency bands (e.g., alpha or theta rhythms). This interplay manifests as a trade-off
between global integrative states and local, information-rich coding states—a redistribution
of the brain’s total coherence budget.
Empirically, this law predicts that increases in coherence within one band or population
are dynamically mirrored by decreases elsewhere, a phenomenon observed in cross-frequency
coupling and neural population coding. Memory formation and recall, perceptual binding,
and even the dynamics of consciousness itself are argued to arise from the lawful flow of
coherence across neural manifolds.
6.7 A Universal Principle of Information Dynamics
The law of coherence conservation provides a unifying framework for understanding mea￾surement, memory, and information flow across scales. It subsumes the uncertainty principle
37

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 38 / 198>

as a special case and extends to any system—physical, biological, or technological—where
information is realized as structured coherence.
From the collapse of a quantum wavefunction to the firing of a neural assembly, the act
of extracting information is always the act of redistributing coherence. In SIT, this principle
replaces the notion of information loss with a more fundamental law: coherence, as the true
substance of information, can only change form, never vanish. This perspective not only
harmonizes quantum and neural theories but also opens new avenues for the design and
analysis of coherent information systems in artificial intelligence, computation, and beyond.
6.8 Coherence as the Substrate of Information
In SIT, information is identified with structured patterns of coherence. The wavefunction’s
off-diagonal elements, the degree of phase alignment in superpositions, and the macroscopic
synchrony of oscillating fields—all of these instantiate physical coherence. Each act of mea￾surement, whether performed by a photodetector, an electron microscope, or a network of
neurons, is fundamentally an act of extracting coherence in a particular basis.
A measurement yielding precise information in one domain corresponds to maximal ex￾traction of coherence from that domain; the system is projected onto a sharply defined state.
By necessity, this process diminishes coherence in the conjugate domain. The quantum state
cannot remain sharply defined in both position and momentum, nor in both time and energy,
nor (in the neural case) in both high-frequency and low-frequency oscillatory synchrony. Co￾herence is not destroyed—it is redistributed. The uncertainty principle is thus not merely a
restriction but a reflection of coherence conservation.
6.9 The Measurement–Disturbance Relationship Reframed
Conventional quantum mechanics regards the measurement process as a source of indetermi￾nacy and randomness, the so-called “collapse” of the wavefunction. In the SIT framework,
measurement is reinterpreted as the dynamic reallocation of a finite coherence budget be￾tween complementary observables. Let CA and CB denote quantitative measures of coherence
in observables A and B (such as position and momentum). The act of measurement enforces
a relationship of the form
CA CB ∼ constant, (13)
where the precise nature of the constant is dictated by the algebraic structure of the conjugate
pair (often set by ℏ). This expresses a coherence-conservation law: sharpening coherence in
A necessarily broadens, or decoheres, B.
Information extraction becomes the physical act of transferring coherence from one basis
to another—an operation that cannot be performed globally and simultaneously across all
complementary bases due to the unitary structure of quantum theory. Thus, SIT reframes
measurement disturbance not as epistemic violence, but as a lawful flow and redistribution
of coherence, revealing a deeper symmetry at the heart of quantum information.
38

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 39 / 198>

6.10 Uncertainty as Coherence Trade-Off: A Unified Perspective
From the SIT standpoint, the Heisenberg uncertainty principle is the paradigmatic instance
of a broader conservation law governing the extraction and redistribution of information in
physical systems. This law generalizes beyond quantum mechanics. In oscillatory neural
systems, for example, the extraction of precise, information-rich patterns in high-frequency
bands (such as gamma oscillations) is accompanied by decreased coherence in lower-frequency
background rhythms, and vice versa. The information-rich event—whether quantum or neu￾ral—appears as a local spike in coherence, made possible only by a compensatory reduction
elsewhere in the informational field.
Thus, the uncertainty principle is neither an arbitrary quantum limit nor a mere state￾ment about measurement devices; it is a consequence of coherence conservation, enforced
whenever a system is probed for maximal information content in a given domain. Measure￾ment, in this framework, is the physical redistribution of coherence—information cannot be
created or destroyed, only relocated between conjugate observables.
6.11 Implications and Experimental Proposals
This reframing of the measurement–disturbance relationship carries empirical consequences.
In quantum experiments, the explicit tracking of coherence (via, for example, weak measure￾ment protocols or quantum tomography) should reveal a precise trade-off in the coherence
budget between conjugate variables, beyond what is predicted by probability alone. In neu￾ral systems, simultaneous recordings of high- and low-frequency synchrony during sensory
processing or memory retrieval should reveal dynamic, reciprocal shifts in coherence, in line
with the predictions of SIT.
In sum, SIT advances the claim that the uncertainty principle is a physical manifesta￾tion of the deeper law of coherence conservation. This law bridges quantum physics and
neuroscience, providing a unifying language for understanding how information is measured,
disturbed, and conserved across the universe’s physical substrates.

7 Quantum Operator Formalism for the Informational

nature of reality in a mathematically rigorous and self-consistent formalism. This section
outlines the canonical quantization of the SIT fields and demonstrates the equivalence of the
resulting quantum, path-integral, and classical descriptions.

7.3 Supersymmetric Structure, Stability, and the Riemann Zeros
To ensure the physical stability and non-negative energy spectrum of the informational sub￾strate, a requirement for any viable physical theory (see Section 16), we postulate that the
SIT Hamiltonian, Hˆ
SIT, possesses a fundamental supersymmetric structure, inspired by su￾persymmetric quantum mechanics [?, ?, ?]. We postulate that the Hamiltonian operator
governing the dynamics of the informational fields is not fundamental, but possesses a fac￾torizable structure, derivable from a more primitive, non-Hermitian operator, Aˆ, and its
adjoint, Aˆ†
.
Hˆ

We identify Aˆ as the operator of informational dissipation or coherence collapse. It
represents the physical process by which informational excitations are annihilated, driving
the system towards a stable vacuum state.
This supersymmetric factorization is not merely a mathematical convenience; it has pro￾found physical consequences that provide a first-principles, non-perturbative guarantee of
the theory’s stability:
1. Guaranteed Stability: By construction, the expectation value of the Hamiltonian is
always non-negative: ⟨Ψ|Hˆ
SIT|Ψ⟩ = ⟨Ψ|Aˆ†Aˆ|Ψ⟩ = ||Aˆ|Ψ⟩||2 ≥ 0. This mathematically
guarantees that all energy eigenvalues are non-negative (E ≥ 0), rigorously precluding
the existence of unphysical ’ghost’ states with negative kinetic energy. This provides
a first-principles mechanism for the stability of the SIT vacuum, a core requirement
outlined in Section 16, ensuring the theory is free from tachyonic instabilities and has
a stable ground state.

2. Special Status of Zero-Energy States: This structure provides a deep physical
origin for the stable modes of the theory. A zero-energy ground state (E = 0) exists if

Aˆ|Ψ0⟩ = 0 (24)
These states are the supersymmetric vacua of the theory, corresponding to the stable
vacua of the informational universe. They are not merely the lowest energy states, but
states of perfect, non-dissipative balance.
Connection to the Arithmetic Sector: This framework provides a deep and direct
physical basis for the role of the Riemann zeta function in SIT. As will be detailed in
the Arithmetic Sector (Section 47), following operator-theoretic approaches to the Riemann
Hypothesis [?], we identify the condition for a supersymmetric vacuum, Aˆ|Ψ0⟩ = 0, as being
mathematically equivalent to the statement ζ(s) = 0.
The non-trivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function therefore correspond to the specific,
discrete configurations of the informational fields that define the stable, non-dissipative vac￾uum states of reality. The Riemann Hypothesis is thus reframed as a physical statement
about the existence and properties of these fundamental vacua. This supersymmetric struc￾ture transforms the requirement for stability from a separate condition into an intrinsic,
architectural feature of the theory, while simultaneously elevating the Riemann zeros from a
phenomenological correspondence or mathematical curiosity to a foundational pillar defining
the universe’s most stable informational states.

not separate entities but emerge from the rich geometry of the single, underlying coherence
field, ψ(x) = Rcoh(x)e

. We demonstrate that electromagnetism and gravity arise as
distinct, orthogonal geometric consequences of this field’s phase and modulus, respectively.

gauge symmetry of the SIT action (Section 5.2), the coherence phase θ(x) gives rise to a
natural connection field, which we identify with the electromagnetic four-potential Aµ:
Aµ(x) := ℏ

Fµν := ∂µAν − ∂νAµ =

its decoupling from gravity (Section 25.X).

Abstract
Albert Einstein, a renowned figure in the realm of science, is celebrated for his ground-breaking discovery of the 
theory of relativity, aimed at elucidating various cosmological and astrophysical phenomena. This theory stands as the 
cornerstone of modern science, with Einstein's revolutionary insight demonstrating the equivalence of mass and 
energy. This revelation marked a pivotal moment in the field of physics, ushering in a new era of understanding. 
Einstein's theory of relativity paved the way for further advancements in science, leading to the development of 
quantum physics, which sought to unravel the intricacies of particles and atoms. According to Einstein, the speed of 
light remains the ultimate cosmic speed limit, a notion grounded in the constancy of our universe's permittivity and 
permeability. In the context of this research paper, we embark on an exploration to unveil a novel theory. Our objective 
is to ascertain the maximum angular velocity attainable by an object within our universe, along with the formulation 
of this ground-breaking theory. This research is inherently linked to the domain of kinematics, and while it shares 
elements with relativistic kinematics, it distinguishes itself as an entirely fresh and distinctive approach. The theory 
can be applied to cosmology, astrophysics and nuclear physics, and the theory is also introducing a new concept, 
named "Ideal photon." It is imperative to acknowledge that the validity of this theory may yield both correctness and 
fallacy. As researchers and authors, we humbly present our findings for further scrutiny and examination. Thank you 
for your consideration.

governs the intricacies of objects in motion, aiming to elucidate its fundamental principles. Angular velocity, a key

significance in the broader context of our universe's physical laws. Additionally, we shall investigate the intricate 
relationship between angular velocity and its counterpart, linear velocity, shedding light on the profound interplay 
between these two essential components of motion. A defining moment in this research paper is the introduction of an 
innovative theory. We will unveil a formula designed to uncover the maximum angular velocities achievable by objects 
within our universe. This ground-breaking theory promises to expand our understanding of motion to new horizons, 
potentially reshaping how we perceive the limits of physical motion. To aid in the comprehension and validation of 
our findings, we will employ graph analysis, providing visual insights into the theoretical underpinnings of our

implications and significance of our research's outcomes. Finally, we draw this intellectual expedition to a close with

scientific community. Throughout this paper, we uphold the highest standards of rigor and precision, aiming to 
contribute to the ever-evolving tapestry of scientific knowledge. As we embark on this intellectual voyage, we invite

inquiry. With unwavering dedication to the pursuit of knowledge, we present this research paper as a beacon of
curiosity and innovation.
2. Introduction to Motion
Motion refers to the displacement of an object, body, or particle from one position to another within a defined reference 
frame [10]. In the realm of physics, the study of motion falls under the purview of kinematics, which is primarily 
concerned with elucidating the intricacies of object movement. This comprehensive field encompasses essential 
parameters such as speed, velocity and acceleration. Within the vast spectrum of motion, several distinct categories 
can be observed [10]:
I) Rectilinear motion: This particular mode of motion is characterized by the traversal of a straight, uncurved 
trajectory, devoid of any alterations in direction. A classic example is a car moving seamlessly along a straight track.
II) Rotational motion: In the realm of rotational motion, objects pivot or spin around a defined axis. Common 
instances include the rotation of a spherical ball or the spinning of a wheel [10].
III) Circular motion: Objects engaged in circular motion trace a circular path around a fixed point. The orbital motion 
of planets and the lunar journey exemplify this type of movement.
IV) Oscillatory motion: Oscillatory motion is typified by the rhythmic to-and-fro oscillations of an object around a 
central point. Examples encompass the graceful sway of a pendulum and the melodious vibrations of a guitar string 
[10].
V) Translational motion: Translational motion manifests as the unadulterated displacement of an entity from one 
geographical point to another, free from any rotational component. A quintessential illustration is the descent of a fruit 
from a tree.
VI) Periodic motion: This distinctive form of motion unfolds with the repetition of a specific movement within fixed 
time intervals [9]. The periodicity is exemplified by phenomena such as the oscillations of a vibrating string, the 
harmonious strumming of a guitar, or the rhythmic swing of a pendulum [10].
The exploration of motion lies at the heart of comprehending the physical universe. By meticulously observing and 
dissecting these various facets of motion, scientists and researchers acquire the capacity to formulate insightful 
predictions about the behavior of objects and the intricate tapestry of the world that surrounds us [10].

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 3 / 15>

3. Introduction to Kinematics 
Kinematics, a fundamental branch of physics, delves into the analysis of object motion while disregarding the forces 
instigating that motion [2]. It focuses on elucidating an object's position, velocity and acceleration as it traverses 
through both space and time. Kinematics holds paramount significance in both classical and contemporary physics, 
serving as the underpinning for numerous other disciplines, including mechanics, astronomy and robotics. Within 
kinematics, an object's position is elucidated in relation to specific coordinates with respect to a chosen reference frame
[2]. For instance, envision a car traveling along a straight road; one can describe its location as the distance from a 
fixed point on the road and its position along the road itself [3]. Velocity, in the context of kinematics, denotes the rate 
of positional change over time and is determined through the derivative of the position function. Acceleration, 
conversely, characterizes the rate of velocity alteration with respect to time and is derived from the velocity function
[3]. Kinematics plays an instrumental role in resolving various motion-related problems. For example, given the initial 
velocity of a ball, kinematics empowers us to ascertain its travel distance and the time required to attain a specific 
height or distance. Similarly, when a car is traveling at a constant speed and decelerating at a known rate, kinematics 
aids in calculating the time it takes to come to a complete halt. Among the pivotal concepts in kinematics, motion in 
one dimension stands out. This arises when an object moves exclusively along a linear path, simplifying more intricate 
scenarios. However, kinematics can be employed to characterize motion in two or three dimensions, offering a more 
realistic portrayal of physical phenomena [2]. Another critical facet of kinematics is projectile motion, which 
encompasses the movement of an object propelled into the air before descending to the ground. This concept holds 
substantial importance in fields like ballistics, enabling precise trajectory calculations for projectiles like bullets or 
rockets [2]. In summary, kinematics is the cornerstone of motion analysis, focusing solely on an object's position, 
velocity, and acceleration while disregarding the forces governing its movement. It serves as a linchpin in solving 
motion-related problems and holds profound relevance in diverse fields, including mechanics, astronomy, and 
robotics. This discipline forms the bedrock for advanced topics like dynamics, which factor in the forces steering 
motion and find application in domains like robotics, where they guide the programming of robotic movements [3].
4. A Description of Angular Velocity 
Angular velocity, a fundamental concept in physics, serves as a means to quantify the rate of change in an object's 
angular position concerning time [10]. This parameter encapsulates the speed at which an object rotates or moves 
within a circular trajectory and is typically expressed in units of radians per second [3]. Crucially, angular velocity is 
a vector quantity, characterized by both magnitude and direction. The magnitude corresponds to the rate of angular 
alteration, while the direction adheres to the right-hand rule and remains perpendicular to the plane of rotation. To 
compute angular velocity, one can ascertain it by dividing the change in an object's angular position by the time interval 
over which this change transpires [4]. The formula delineating angular velocity takes the form:
ω = Δθ/Δt (1)
In this equation, ω symbolizes angular velocity, Δθ signifies the alteration in angular position, and Δt denotes the 
duration over which this change unfolds [4]. Angular velocity occupies a pivotal role in the realm of physics, 
specifically for characterizing the movement of objects engaged in rotation or circular motion. It finds application in 
calculating the rotational speed of various components like gears and wheels, as well as in elucidating the dynamics 
of celestial bodies, such as planets, as they traverse space [4]. Beyond physics, this concept holds practical utility in 
engineering endeavors, notably in the design of turbines and other rotating machinery. Moreover, it proves 
indispensable in fields like robotics, where precise control over rotating elements is paramount [10].

6. Discovery of New Theory to Measure the Maximum Angular Velocity of Objects in Our Universe
The speed of light in a vacuum is constant and is approximately 299,792,458 metres per second (or about 186,282
miles per second) [11]. This is a fundamental principle in physics and is represented by the symbol "c." It means that
light always travels at this speed, regardless of the motion of the source or the observer [1].
This concept is a cornerstone of Albert Einstein's theory of special relativity, which states that the laws of physics are
the same for all observers, and the speed of light is the maximum speed at which information or energy can be
transmitted throughout the universe [1]. This constant speed of light has been confirmed by extensive experimental
evidence and is one of the key principles of modern physics. Therefore, the relationship between angular velocity and
linear velocity is given by [4],
 v = rω
 ω =
v
r
 ωmax =
vmax
r
 (For vmax we will obtain ωmax and vmax is the maximum velocity of objects in our universe)
 ωmax =
c
r
 (As the speed of light is the maximum velocity in our universe, c equates to vmax)
:. ωmax =
c
r
 (3)
Fig.1: Circular Path
Let's consider a scenario where an object is in motion along a circular path, and we identify the central point of this
circular path as O. The radius of this circular path is r1 (r1 = OA). Let's picture an orbit with that same radius, which
we'll designate as P. Within this orbit, the maximum angular velocity is represented as ωmax-1 = c/(r1). It's crucial to
emphasise that should the angular velocity of this object exceed ωmax-1, it implies that the object is moving at a speed
greater than the speed of light in terms of linear velocity. This would, in turn, conflict with the theory of relativity [11].
According to the theory of relativity, the speed of light is the utmost velocity achievable within our universe.

{'asset_pointer': 'file-service://file-K1L84WPu94Kopq7zaCfEZt', 'content_type': 'image_asset_pointer', 'fovea': None, 'height': 1024, 'metadata': None, 'size_bytes': 128795, 'width': 768}

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 5 / 15>

Nevertheless, if we contemplate a different orbit with an alternative radius, let's say r2 (r2 = OB) and name this orbit
as Q, the maximum angular velocity within Q will be ωmax-2 = c/(r2). We observe that, as r1 is not equal to r2, ωmax-1
will not equate to ωmax-2. However, it's noteworthy that the product of ωmax-2 and r2 will be equivalent to c, and the
product of ωmax-1 and r1 will also be equal to c [1]. The same principle applies to orbits with various radii.
Consequently, we can summarise as follows:
Given that,
r1 ≠ r2 ≠ r3 ≠ r4 ≠ ... ≠ rn, ωmax-1 ≠ ωmax-2 ≠ ωmax-3 ≠ ωmax-4 ≠ ... ≠ ωmax-n . As each radius differs, the maximum angular
velocities within these orbits will not be uniform.
Nonetheless,
(ωmax-1 x r1) = (ωmax-2 x r2) = (ωmax-3 x r3) = (ωmax-4 x r4) = ... = (ωmax-n x rn) = c = vmax (4)
Hence, we can introduce our theory, which has been coined the 'Max Omega Theory.'
Max Omega Theory (MOT): The maximum angular velocity of an object moving in a circular path is the quotient
of speed of light (c) and the radius of the circular path.
Or, The product of maximum angular velocity of a object moving in a circular path and the radius of circular path is
equal to the speed of light.
The theory can function within the realms of classical physics, quantum physics and find applications in astronomy
and astrophysics.

can achieve such high speeds, while massive objects cannot [1].

governing the universe. Whether in classical physics, where we investigate the fundamental forces and motions of 
everyday objects, or in the realm of quantum physics, where we delve into the enigmatic world of particles and their 
quantum states, theory proves to be of pragmatic significance. Furthermore, its application extends into the vast 
cosmos, with astronomy and astrophysics heavily reliant on theoretical models to decipher celestial phenomena. The 
significance of this wide-ranging application cannot be overstated. It serves as the linchpin connecting abstract 
concepts to tangible accomplishments. Through these applications, we pioneer new technologies, enhance existing 
ones, and extend the boundaries of our knowledge. Minus the practical application of theory, our scientific progress 
would be greatly impeded. Essentially, the application of theory serves as the catalyst for innovation, fostering 
breakthroughs with far-reaching impacts on society. It acts as the driving force behind advancements in healthcare, 
energy, communications, and numerous other fields, improving our quality of life and broadening the horizons of 
human understanding. It is the bridge that transforms theoretical knowledge into concrete progress, rendering it an 
indispensable facet of our scientific and technological journey.
8.1 Application of MOT in Nuclear Physics

The hydrogen atom holds a special place in the world of science as the simplest and most basic building block of 
matter. At its core, this atom is composed of two fundamental components: a nucleus and an electron. The nucleus, 
residing at the atom's centre, consists of a single proton. This proton carries a positive electric charge and contains 
nearly all of the atom's mass. Orbiting around this nucleus is a lone electron, which is negatively charged and 
remarkably lighter than its proton counterpart [7].
In the hydrogen atom, the electron exists in specific energy levels or orbits around the nucleus. These orbits are often 
depicted as concentric circles, but they are more accurately described as three-dimensional regions where the electron 
is likely to be found.
One of the most intriguing aspects of the hydrogen atom is the behaviour of its electron [7]. When the electron absorbs 
or releases energy, it can transition from one energy level to another. These transitions lead to the emission or 
absorption of light, giving hydrogen its characteristic spectral lines of coloured light. What sets the hydrogen atom 
apart is its simplicity. This simplicity makes it a fundamental model for understanding atomic structure and behaviour. 
Many of the principles underpinning quantum mechanics and atomic physics were first developed through the study 
of the hydrogen atom [7].
Moreover, hydrogen holds great significance in the cosmos. It is the most abundant element in the universe, 
constituting approximately 75% of all elemental mass. It plays a pivotal role in the nuclear fusion that occurs within 
stars, including our own sun. On the periodic table, hydrogen occupies the first position with the chemical symbol H. 
Its ubiquity, simplicity, and role in foundational scientific research make the hydrogen atom a cornerstone in our 
understanding of the physical world and the universe itself. Its atomic number Z is equal to 1 [7].

8.3 Employing MOT to Theorise Ideal Photon.
All electromagnetic waves are quantised in the sense that they are composed of individual packets of energy known 
as "Photons." These photons are quantised particles, meaning they come in discrete, specific energy levels determined 
by the frequency of the electromagnetic wave [6]. This concept is a fundamental aspect of quantum mechanics and is 
described by Planck's equation, where the energy of a photon (E) is directly proportional to its frequency (ν) through 
the constant h (Planck's constant): E = hν. In practical terms, this quantisation means that the energy carried by 
electromagnetic waves, such as light, is not continuous but comes in distinct units, with each unit represented by a 
single photon. The energy of each photon is determined by the frequency of the wave it belongs to [6]. As the frequency 
increases, the energy of the photons also increases. So, while electromagnetic waves appear continuous in everyday 
experience, at the quantum level, they are inherently quantised because they consist of discrete photons. This property 
has profound implications in quantum physics and our understanding of the behaviour of light and other 
electromagnetic phenomena. We know that Planck's length is the smallest theoretically possible length in the universe, 
below which the concepts of space and length lose their classical meaning. It is approximately 1.616 x 10-35 meters. 
This fundamental length scale is derived from fundamental constants of nature, such as the speed of light, Planck's 
constant, and the gravitational constant, and is a key concept in theoretical physics, particularly in the context of 
quantum mechanics and the search for a theory of quantum gravity. However, we can derive the equation of the " Ideal 
photon." Therefore, the relationship with frequency and maximum angular velocity is given by,
 (ωmax-n x rn) = f λ = c
We observe that when rn = lp (Planck's length), then ωmax-n = 1.85 x 1043 rad/sec (according to equation no.6).
Furthermore, when λ = lp, then f = 1.85 x 1043 Hz. Moreover, the ratio of ωmax-n and f is equal to the ratio of λ and rn
according to this equation.
[Radius is 1-dimensional and is a type of length. An orbit with a 0 m radius has no entity; the smallest orbit's radius
will be equal to the smallest length in the universe, considering Planck's length as the smallest meaningful length (for
circle path). We can also state that the minimum value will be equal to the Planck's length]
 ωmax-n ÷ f = λ ÷ rn = k 
If we insert the values, we will obtain the value of k. Hence,
 1.85×1043÷1.85 x 1043 = lp ÷ lp = k =1
Therefore, k = 1.
However, for making the concept easy, we will simply utilise, f λ = c
 E = hv = hc/λ (16) 

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 10 / 15>

We notice that as λ approaches to lp (Planck's length), photons get the maximum frequency in this universe. According 
to v = c/λ, we can express this as vmax = c/lp. For the smallest wavelength, a photon will have the highest frequency. 
Moreover, the wavelength can't be smaller than Planck's length. Let's find out the highest frequency of photon. So,
 vmax = c/lp = 1.856×1043 Hz (Theoretical)
Furthermore, the energy of an Ideal photon goes as,
 EIdeal photon = hvmax = 12,300,742,550.64 J = 1.23 x 1010 J (Theoretical) (17)
Hence, from 1 mole Ideal photons we obtain,
 U = 6.023 x 1023 x 12,300,742,550.64 = 7.4 x 1033 J (Theoretical)
Ultimately, we obtained the definition of Ideal photon.
Ideal photon: The photon whose wavelength is equal to the smallest length in the universe, or the photon with the 
highest frequency in the universe, is referred to as the Ideal photon.
In accordance with current quantum physics, it is entirely theoretical. If we were to discover a photon in real-life 
exhibiting a frequency greater than vmax and possessing more energy than that of an ideal photon, it would affirm that 
the wavelength of such a photon is smaller than the Planck's length. Moreover, this discovery would substantiate that 
Planck's length is not the ultimate minimum length in our universe. Since it no longer holds this distinction, the 
equations that are built upon the assumption of Planck's length being the smallest will become invalid, leading to chaos 
within the realm of physics. Consequently, we would need to revise and reconstruct the fundamental theories of 
physics from their very foundations.
9 Using the Ideal Photon for Measuring the Efficiency of Photons
There are various types of electromagnetic wave [6].
I) Gamma Rays:
 - Frequency: Greater than 3 x 1019 Hz.
 - Wavelength: Less than 0.01 nm.
 - Gamma rays possess the highest frequencies within the electromagnetic spectrum and are exceptionally energetic.
They are typically associated with nuclear reactions and certain types of radioactive decay.
II) X-Rays:
 - Frequency: Approximately 3 x 1016 Hz to 3 x 1019 Hz.
 - Wavelength: Approximately 0.01 nm to 10 nm.
 - X-rays have frequencies that fall between ultraviolet and gamma rays. They find extensive use in medical imaging
and the examination of internal structures of materials.
III) Ultraviolet (UV) Light:
 - Frequency: Approximately 7.5 x 1014 Hz to 3 x 1016 Hz.
 - Wavelength: Approximately 10 nm to 400 nm.
 - Ultraviolet light has higher frequencies than visible light and is responsible for various biological and chemical
effects, such as skin tanning and the sterilization of air and water.
IV) Visible Light:
 - Frequency: Approximately 4 x 1014 Hz to 7.5 x 1014 Hz.
 - Wavelength: Approximately 400 nm to 700 nm.
 - Visible light constitutes the range of electromagnetic radiation that our eyes can perceive. It enables our sense of
sight and comprises various colours, each with a specific frequency.
V) Infrared (IR) Radiation:
 - Frequency: Approximately 3 x 1011 Hz to 4 x 1014 Hz.

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 11 / 15>

 - Wavelength: Approximately 700 nm to 1 mm.
 - Infrared radiation is frequently employed in applications like remote controls, thermal imaging, and cooking due
to its heat-producing characteristics.
VI) Microwaves:
 - Frequency: Approximately 3 x 109 Hz to 3 x 1011 Hz.
 - Wavelength: Approximately 1mm to 1 m.
 - Microwaves are utilised in microwave ovens and various communication technologies, including radar and satellite
communication.
VII) Radio Waves:
 - Frequency: Less than 3 x 109 Hz.
 - Wavelength: Greater than 1 m.
 - Radio waves possess the lowest frequencies within the electromagnetic spectrum. They are employed in radio and
television broadcasting, as well as wireless communication systems.
These are some of the principal categories of electromagnetic waves, along with their approximate frequency ranges.
The electromagnetic spectrum is continuous, encompassing many more divisions within these general categories.
However, the efficiency of an engine pertains to its ability to convert energy from a fuel source into practical
mechanical work while minimizing energy losses, typically in the form of waste heat. In practical terms, it measures
how effectively the engine can perform its intended function, such as powering a vehicle or generating electricity, with
the least amount of fuel or input energy. Higher engine efficiency means that a greater percentage of the energy from
the fuel is transformed into useful work, which is desirable for improving performance and reducing environmental
impact. In the context of photon efficiency, it represents the percentage ratio of a photon's energy to the ideal photon.
The efficiency of a photon of electromagnetic wave follows, (Later it has been named as "The level of a photon,")
 η(Name of electromagnetic wave) = (EPhoton ÷ EIdeal photon) x 100% (18)
Where,
 η(Name of electromagnetic wave) = Efficiency of a photon of electromagnetic wave;
 EPhoton = Energy of photon;
 EIdeal photon = Energy of ideal photon.
To determine the efficiency of a gamma-ray photon, we must ascertain its frequency. The gamma-ray frequency range
is 3 x 1020 Hz or higher. Let us consider a photon which holding a frequency of 3×1020 Hz. Therefore,
η(Gamma-ray) = (EPhoton ÷ EIdeal photon) x 100%

9.1 Using the Ideal Photon of MOT To Introduce the Photonic Bomb
An atomic bomb, also known as a nuclear bomb, is a highly destructive weapon that utilises the power of nuclear
fission or fusion reactions to generate an explosive force of immense magnitude. Its name is derived from the fact that
it depends on the splitting (fission) or merging (fusion) of atomic nuclei. In a fission bomb, like the ones dropped on
Hiroshima and Nagasaki during World War II, the nucleus of a heavy element such as uranium-235 or plutonium-239
is divided, releasing an enormous amount of energy. This process is accompanied by a chain reaction, where each split
nucleus triggers further splits, resulting in a devastating explosion. In a fusion bomb, also known as a hydrogen bomb,
two light atomic nuclei, typically isotopes of hydrogen, combine to create a heavier nucleus, releasing even more
energy than fission alone. The destructive potential of atomic bombs makes them among the deadliest weapons ever
created, with catastrophic consequences for both immediate and long-term human and environmental health.
However, the new concept named the "Photonic bomb" or "Quantum bomb" is based on the concept of an ideal photon.
A single ideal photon carries an immense amount of energy, and the energy of 1 mole of ideal photons is equal to a
8.2 x 1013 ton massive object if it could be converted into energy according to the mass-energy equivalency. Ideal
photons are not currently possible to create, but it is theoretically possible to increase photon energy closer to that of
an ideal photon. A photonic bomb would emit enormous photons, each containing energy approaching that of an ideal
photon, potentially making it the deadliest bomb in history. We do not encourage the construction of such a device
for the purpose of warfare. Instead, we should strive for peace and cooperation. However, for the sake of scientific
knowledge, it may be worth exploring whether such a concept is feasible.
9.2 The Concept of the Ideal Photon Discovers a New Principle in Cosmology
Cosmology is the scientific exploration of the universe's origin, structure, development, and ultimate destiny. It delves
into the vast-scale properties of the entire universe. The connection between cosmology and the Big Bang theory holds
immense significance. The Big Bang theory stands as the predominant cosmological framework elucidating the
universe's genesis [8]. It proposes that the universe commenced as a singularity, an infinitesimal point characterized
by immense density and temperature. From this initial moment, approximately 13.8 billion years ago, the universe has
undergone continual expansion. Substantial proof substantiates the Big Bang theory. Among the pieces of evidence is
the cosmic microwave background radiation, a faint residual glow originating from the Big Bang itself. Moreover, the
observation of redshift in distant galaxies signifies their receding motion, affirming the universe's expansion. In
essence, cosmology scrutinizes the universe's configuration and chronicles, with the Big Bang theory representing a
pivotal concept within cosmology [8]. It expounds upon the universe's inception and expansion, buttressed by
observational data such as the cosmic background radiation and redshift measurements. The "singularity problem" in
cosmology pertains to a theoretical juncture in the universe's history, specifically at the instance of the Big Bang [8],
where the universe's density and temperature become infinitely high. This concept arises from the mathematical
formulations utilised to describe the early universe within the framework of the Big Bang theory [8]. However, the
terminology "singularity" presents a predicament as it implies a breakdown in our comprehension of physics. At such
elevated densities and temperatures, the established laws of physics, encompassing those of general relativity and
quantum mechanics, cease to be applicable. Consequently, our current scientific models are incapable of accurately
elucidating the events at that juncture. To tackle this conundrum, physicists and cosmologists are actively engaged in
researching and crafting theories aimed at providing a more comprehensive account of the universe's behaviour during
its earliest moments. These theories aspire to reconcile the principles of general relativity and quantum mechanics,
furnishing a more all-encompassing understanding of the universe's genesis. Some of these theories, such as
inflationary cosmology, endeavour to ameliorate the singularity problem by proposing that the universe experienced
swift expansion in its nascent stages [8]. In summary, the singularity problem underscores the requisite for a more

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 13 / 15>

encompassing theory pertaining to the universe's inception and evolution, particularly under the extreme circumstances
characterising the Big Bang. On the other hand, it is also noteworthy that the current understanding of physics breaks
down below the Planck's length. The debate over whether space is continuous or discrete is ongoing. In this ongoing
debate, the concept of the Ideal photon has provided a solution. Let's consider a length that is 10 times larger than the
Planck's length, denoted as L = 10 x lp. It's important to note that L is larger than the Planck's length but not
significantly so. To avoid the problem of singularity and the Planck's length (where everything breaks down), we have

Great idea---and I'll run with it. (Tiny spelling note: it's "Leibniz.")
Below is a compact, rigorous "Gold Standard Equivalence Action Plan" (GSEAP) you
can apply to Newton--Leibniz. It gives you two usable thresholds---7 points
("smoking‑gun cluster") and 30 points (full gold‑standard)---plus a π‑calculus
bisimulation sketch and two category‑theory equivalence proofs. I also include the
probability math to justify why a tight multi‑point match is extremely unlikely to be
accidental.
1. What counts as a "point" of equivalence?
A point is an independent, compositional invariant that both systems satisfy. Each point
must be:
1. precisely stated (axiom, rule, translation, or semantic law),
2. testable on arbitrary inputs, and
3. compositional (closed under substitution/contexts), so it isn't a one‑off
coincidence.
We classify points into four tiers (to weight them later if you want Bayes factors):
● Tier A (core calculus laws): chain rule, product rule, linearity, FTC, etc.

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 2 / 56>

● Tier B (derived rules/schemes): quotient rule, implicit/inverse differentiation,
Taylor, L'Hôpital.
● Tier C (multivariate/geometry): Jacobian functoriality, change of variables,
tangent/cotangent structure.
● Tier D (procedural/operational): solution schemes for ODEs, series methods,
error control patterns.
1. Minimal "7‑point smoking‑gun" set
Pick any 7 that are independent and cover both differentiation and integration. Here's a
clean canonical set:
1. Linearity of D: D[af+bg] = a-D[f] + b-D[g].
2. Product rule: D[f-g] = f-D[g] + g-D[f].
3. Chain rule: D[f∘g] = (D[f]∘g)-D[g].
4. Power rule (all rational exponents): D[x^p] = p-x^(p-1).
5. Higher derivatives/Taylor schema: existence of Dⁿ and the Taylor expansion
pattern around a point.
6. Fundamental Theorem of Calculus (FTC): D[∫ f] = f and ∫D[f] = f + C.
7. First‑order ODE capability: a method that, given dy/dx = F(x,y) with y(x₀)=y₀,
recovers the same solution class.
Point 8 (meta‑signature): "The set of these 7 matched invariants forms a unique cluster"
(your insight). Statistically, a coherent cluster of seven independent Tier‑A/B invariants
matching together functions like a "verbatim sentence" in stylometry---far rarer than
seven isolated matches.
2. Extended "30‑point gold standard" (example rubric)
These are granular, independently checkable invariants you can tick off. Use any 30
that are actually independent given your formalization.
Core (A)
1. Linearity of D.
2. Product rule.
3. Chain rule.
4. Power rule for ℚ exponents.
5. D[const]=0 and D[id]=1.

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 3 / 56>

6. D respects addition/multiplication limits (compatibility with limit laws).
7. FTC (both directions, up to constants).
8. Integration by substitution (u‑sub).
9. Integration by parts.

11. Implicit differentiation rule.
12.Inverse function derivative rule.
13.L'Hôpital's schema (as a consequence of Taylor/limits).

17) Change of variables with Jacobian determinant in integrals.
18) Directional derivatives as linear maps; equivalently, differential dfₓ: V→W.
19) Hessian symmetry for C² functions (Clairaut/Schwarz theorem conditions).
20) Tangent functor behavior: T(f∘g)=T(f)∘T(g).

30) Equivalence of normalization conventions for constants of integration and initial

(If you prefer a different 30, swap in items like curvature of plane curves, arc length/area

3. The translation maps (Newton ↔ Leibniz)

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 4 / 56>

Let t denote Newton's time parameter. Newton's "fluxion" of x is ẋ = dx/dt (modern
notation). Leibniz's derivative dy/dx is the ratio of fluxions:
● L→N: (dy/dx) ↦ ẏ/ẋ, and Dₓ = (1/ẋ)-D_t.
● N→L: ẏ ↦ (dy/dx)-ẋ; similarly, ẍ ↦ d/dx(ẋ)-ẋ.
For integrals: Newton's quadrature of f with respect to x corresponds to Leibniz's ∫ f(x)
dx. Under x=x(t), ∫ f(x) dx ↔ ∫ f(x(t))-ẋ dt.
Under these maps:
● Product rule, chain rule, and FTC are preserved (check directly by substitution).
● Any proof carried out in one notation transports to the other by applying the map
homomorphically to subexpressions (compositionality).
4. π‑calculus bisimulation sketch (operational equivalence)
Model each calculus as a process that, when given a context and an expression, emits
labelled transitions for rule applications and outputs a normal form plus an audit trail.
● Channels: der, int, chain, prod, subst, limit, emit.
● Processes:
○ P_L[f] (Leibniz): on input ⟨ctx, f⟩, it performs a sequence of rule‑labels
(e.g., chain, prod) and sends emit⟨value, trace⟩.
○ P_N[f] (Newton): identical interface; internally uses ẋ, ẏ and time‑based
rules.
● Context/process observers can only "see" labels and final numeric/function
outputs.
Bisimulation relation R: relate states (σ_L, σ_N) if (i) they hold expressions e_L, e_N
that are translations of each other via the maps above and (ii) they are in corresponding
contexts.
● Step condition: if σ_L → σ_L′ with label ℓ, then σ_N → σ_N′ with the same label ℓ
(or a stutter‑equivalent micro‑sequence that composes to ℓ), and vice versa, with
(σ_L′, σ_N′) ∈ R.
● Observation condition: final outputs are equal in the semantic model (ℝ or
Smooth), and the emitted traces are label‑wise the same modulo α‑conversion of
variable names/notational differences.

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 5 / 56>

Because the translation preserves: (a) rule applicability, (b) rule results, and (c)
composition of rules, P_L and P_N are (strongly) bisimilar under R on all programs built
from the 7 (and hence the 30) invariants.
5. Two category‑theory proofs of equivalence
Proof A (Cartesian Differential Categories / Differential
λ‑semantics)
● Work in Smooth, the category whose objects are finite‑dimensional real vector
spaces (or open subsets of ℝⁿ) and morphisms are smooth maps.
● Smooth carries a differential combinator D with the usual axioms (linearity in the
second argument, chain rule, etc.).
● Present two syntactic categories, L (Leibniz) and N (Newton), whose morphisms
are terms modulo the 7 axioms, and define semantics functors ⟦-⟧_L: L→Smooth
and ⟦-⟧_N: N→Smooth.
● The translation functors F: L→N and G: N→L (Sec. 3) are identity on objects and
send morphisms by rewriting dy/dx ↦ ẏ/ẋ (and inverse).
● Natural isomorphisms η: Id_L ⇒ G∘F and ε: F∘G ⇒ Id_N exist because every
axiom instance commutes under translation (checked on generators:
prod/chain/FTC/etc.).
⇒ L and N are equivalent categories; their semantics in Smooth coincide (same
D). Hence formal equivalence.
Proof B (Tangent Categories / Functorial calculus)
● Let T: Smooth→Smooth be the tangent functor with natural transformations (zero
section, addition, scalar multiplication).
● In Leibniz syntax, D is represented by dfₓ: X→Y as the linear part of T(f). In
Newton's syntax, ẋ gives a curve lift; differentiation is evaluation of T(f) on (x, ẋ).
● Build functors ⟦-⟧_L^T and ⟦-⟧_N^T into the tangent category (Smooth, T). The
translation maps commute with T and its natural transformations.
● Therefore the two presentations yield the same T‑algebra structure and the same
naturality squares.
⇒ The calculi are isomorphic as T‑structured theories (another, independent
categorical route).

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 6 / 56>

Either proof suffices; together they "gold‑plate" the equivalence.
6. Why 7 (or 30) almost never happens by accident
Model "accidental match" H₀ vs "genuine equivalence" H₁. For n independent points with
null‑match probabilities p₁,...,p_n (calibrated ex‑ante), the Bayes factor favoring H₁ is:
BF = ∏ᵢ (1/pᵢ). (Under H₁ we expect each point to match with probability ≈1.)
Examples (compute on log scale when n is large):
● If pᵢ ≤ 0.2 for 7 points: BF ≥ 5⁷. Compute 5⁷ = 5×5⁶ = 5×15 625 = 78 125 (decisive
evidence).
● If pᵢ ≈ 0.1 for 7 points: BF ≈ 10⁷ = 10,000,000 (overwhelming).
● For 30 points with pᵢ ≤ 0.2: log₁₀ BF = 30-log₁₀ 5 ≈ 30-0.69897 ≈ 20.97, i.e., BF ≈
9.3×10²⁰ (astronomical).
Your "8th point = unique cluster" insight is exactly the move from pointwise evidence to
pattern evidence. Clustering reduces the already tiny H₀ probability further because
many alternative systems can hit a few Tier‑B rules, but almost none hit a coherent,
cross‑tier, compositional 7‑pack.
(Caveat: if some points are not independent, down‑weight them or group them; the BF
formula still works with grouped p's.)
7. Worked micro‑example (translation + rules)
Differentiate f(x)=x^x.
Leibniz: let y=x^x. Then ln y = x ln x. Differentiate: (1/y)-dy/dx = ln x + 1. So dy/dx = y(ln
x + 1) = x^x(1+ln x).
Newton: set x=x(t), y=y(t)=x(t)^{x(t)}. Take t‑fluxions: (ẏ/y) = (ẋ/x)-x + ln(x)-ẋ = (1+ln x)-ẋ.
Hence ẏ = y(1+ln x)-ẋ, so (dy/dx) = ẏ/ẋ = x^x(1+ln x).
Same output; same rule labels (log‑diff, chain, prod); bisimulation and both categorical
semantics agree.

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 7 / 56>

of the procedure uses only invariant‑preserving operations (rules translation, semantic
checks, Bayes aggregation), the two runs are bisimilar. Categorically, the procedure is a
functor from "presentations of calculus" to a verdict object; your two runs become
naturally isomorphic functor applications. Thus the method is self‑consistent, and its
own outputs are stable under L↔N translation.

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

1. Translation maps (Leibniz ↔ Newton)
Let t be Newton's time parameter. Fluxions: ẋ := dx/dt, ẏ := dy/dt.
L→N: (dy/dx) ↦ ẏ/ẋ, and Dₓ = (1/ẋ)-D_t.
N→L: ẏ ↦ (dy/dx)-ẋ; more generally, for any f(x), D_t f = (df/dx)-ẋ.
Integrals/quadratures: ∫ f(x) dx ↔ ∫ f(x(t))-ẋ dt.
Domain side conditions: differentiability as needed; ẋ ≠ 0 where dy/dx is formed.
These maps are compositional and homomorphic over +, -, composition.
───────────────────────────────────────────────────────
─────────────────
1. 7‑point "smoking‑gun" set (each point: statement, L↔N check, micro‑trace)
[1] Linearity of D
Statement: D[af+bg] = a-D[f] + b-D[g] for constants a,b.

<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 9 / 56>

L↔N check: D_t is linear; dividing by ẋ preserves linearity ⇒ Dₓ linear.
Trace: [linearity].
[2] Product rule
Statement: D[f-g] = f-D[g] + g-D[f].
L↔N check: (fg)- = f-ġ + g-ḟ; divide by ẋ ⇒ d(fg)/dx = f dg/dx + g df/dx.
Trace: [prod].
[3] Chain rule
Statement: D[f∘g] = (D[f]∘g)-D[g].
L↔N check: d/dt f(g(x(t))) = (f′∘g)-g′-ẋ; divide by ẋ ⇒ Leibniz chain rule.
Trace: [chain].
[4] Power rule for rational exponents
Statement: D[x^p] = p-x^{p-1} for p ∈ ℚ (appropriate domain).
Sketch: integers by induction via [prod]; reciprocals via implicit y^n=x ⇒ n y^{n-1} dy/dx
= 1; combine with [chain]/[prod] for m/n.
L↔N check: same steps with fluxions, then divide by ẋ.
Trace: [prod]→[implicit]→[chain].
[5] Higher derivatives / Taylor schema
Statement: Dⁿ exists where smooth; Taylor expansion pattern holds with standard
remainder conditions.
L↔N check: Repeated application of L rules ↔ repeated fluxions with division by ẋ;
coefficients (n choose k) coincide by [prod]/[chain] combinatorics.
Trace: [chain][prod]×n.
[6] Fundamental Theorem of Calculus (FTC)
Statement: D[∫ f] = f and ∫D[f] = f + C (on connected domains).
L↔N check: If y is a quadrature with ẏ = f(x)-ẋ, then dy/dx = f(x). Conversely, integrate
dy/dx along x to recover y up to constant.
Trace: [def‑of‑quadrature]→[divide‑by‑ẋ] and [FTC].
[7] First‑order ODE capability (representative case)
Statement: dy/dx = F(x,y) solved by same scheme (e.g., separable or linear).
Witness: dy/dx = λ y ⇒ in N: ẏ = λ y ẋ ⇒ (1/y)-ẏ = λ ẋ ⇒ integrate in t ⇒ ln y = λ x + C
⇒ y = C e^{λ x}.
L gives the same.
Trace: [separate‑variables]→[FTC].
Result: 7/7 PASS. The cluster is coherent across differentiation and integration, not a
grab‑bag. This is your "8th meta‑point": the joint pattern itself.

Why it holds\

1. Two category‑theory confirmations
A) Cartesian Differential Categories (or Smooth as a differential category)\
● Build syntax categories L and N from the 7 axioms; semantics functors ⟦-⟧_L,
⟦-⟧_N into Smooth.\
● Define translations F: L→N and G: N→L as in Sec. 0 (identity on objects;
morphisms rewritten dy/dx ↔ ẏ/ẋ).\
● Natural isomorphisms η: Id_L ⇒ G∘F and ε: F∘G ⇒ Id_N exist because
generators/relations commute with the translation.
⇒ L ≃ N (categorical equivalence) and ⟦-⟧_L = ⟦-⟧_N∘F on the nose.
B) Tangent Categories (functor T with its natural transformations)\
● In L, Df at x is dfₓ: X→Y as the linear part of T(f); in N, evaluation on (x,ẋ) is the
same T(f) action.\
● The translations commute with T and its structure maps, giving an isomorphism
of T‑algebras.
⇒ Theories are isomorphic as T‑structured presentations; hence functionally
identical.
